About The Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend
on the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and
Lancaster on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby
on the west. It is about 47 miles from Boston and
29 miles from Worcester
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level

26.63 Square Miles

===PAGE 8===
Board of Selectmen
Growth and change
Growth and change have been the two most prevalent items mentioned in Lunenburg Town Reports for about the
last 15 years. For Lunenburg, 1967 held as much, if not more, of both of these as any of the preceeding years. The
routine day to day business of the office of Selectmen was pretty much the same as in previous years. There were the
usual number of licenses, permits, zoning applications, dog problems, etc., as well as the usual appointments to Town
Committees. However, it was in the not so routine areas that we had our greatest confrontation with growth and change.
The Townspeople in special Town meetings voted to contruct a new school, establish a Housing Authority to
build homes for the aged, and rezone an area in the hopes of attracting an industry to our Town. All of these are
products of the growth of Lunenburg. The change which will have the greatest ramifications on the effectiveness of
future Boards of Selectmen was the result of the resignation of our fine clerk of many years, Mrs. Mary L. Padula,
because of the pressure of her many duties. The office of Selectmen's Clerk which Mary had filled on a part-time basis
was expanded to a full-time position. Mr. Richard J. Eley has beensecured to fill this position and to set upa separate
office for most all of the Selectmen's functions.
The future promises to hold even greater changes in store. The State is scheduled to take over our entire Welfare
Program because of 1967 legislation. The Town must face up to the solution of its ambulance problem and must continue
to seek a solution to its sewerage problems.
During 1967 the Highway Department continued with its program of upgrading drainage systems throughout the
Town as well as maintaining all Town roads. The reconstruction of Leominster Road was started and will continue until
completed. The new street sweeper has been of much aid to this department and even was a hit in the Old Home Days"
parade. Lunenburg's Town Report was once more awarded a First Prize by the Massachusetts Selectmen's Association.
Special appreciation is extended to Town Clerk, Mrs. Padula, and her staff for the fine job they have done through the
years on these reports. For the first time in thirty years Lunenburg held an 'Old Home Days" celebration. Although
strictly speaking, this is not a Town function, it helps to create a great deal of civic interest which in turn benefits
the Town. The Board of Selectmen wholeheartedly endorses any program which achieves greater civic interest.
Board of Selectmen: Bernard J. Paton, Chairman Normand J. Thompson, Carl W. Proctor.
- 6 -
===PAGE 9===
Board of Assessors
Equalization Tax Program Goal
EQUALIZATION of the tax burden is the goal of any tax program. Applying this to the property tax in Lunenburg
means that all classes of property in town should carry their fair and equal share of taxes.
It is the considered opinion of the members of the Board of Assessors that a revaluation program conducted by a
firm of experts is a giant step toward achieving equalization. Therefore, the Board of Assessors is continuing its efforts
to win voter approval of such a program by submitting an article at the March 1968 Town Meeting.
Lunenburg's first and only revaluation program was instituted in 1952-1953. At the time that the results of this
program were incorporated in the property tax program (in 1954) it won the hearty approval of the majority of the
property owners because a much better degree of equalization was achieved. Since 1954, the total valuation of all
property in town has jumped from $7,000,000 to $12,300,000 and the 4441 parcels of property listed in 1967 is
almost double that number listed in 1954. Such increases, coupled with the economic changes that have occurred in
the past 14 years, point to the need for another revaluation program in order to once more bring about a desired degree
of equalization.
Another matter that presently faces the Board of Assessors is that of assessing all property at full-fairmarket value
in accordance with a ruling of the Supreme Court of Massachusetts. The Board believes that this method of assessing
can best be achieved through a revaluation program. Any other method of achieving full-fair assessments would be,
in the minds of the assessors, unsatisfactory.
The many aspects and methods involved in any local tax program create the impression that it is difficult to
comprehend. However, from past experiences in discussing tax problems with individuals, a better understanding has
been achieved. The Board welcomes and urges taxpayers to discuss problems involved in the local tax program.
In 1967 the members of the Worcester County Assessors Association elected a local member, Arthur J. Brockelman,
to serve as president of the Association.
Board of Assessors: Arthur J. Brockelman, Chairman Chester J. Pliska, Clerk-Kathleen M. Deming, Leroy Allen.

Town Counsel
A Guiding Hand
The beginning of each new year generally signals the start of the long drive
toward the annual Town Meeting in March. These matters coupled with the attention
which the routine administration of Town business commands, is usually sufficient to
keep the pressure on right down to the final adjournment of Town Meeting. Something
new was added in 1967, however, the issue of a new school was finally settled after
having been considered for morethan two years. The hotly contested issues of -what?
-where? and -how much? were finally resolved at a special Town Meeting in January,
1967. Considerable painstaking work was necessary to make it possible for voter con-
sideration to be given to all propositions within the framework of the law. Beyond that,
the vote passed necessitated extensive land takings. Thanks to the cooperation of the
land owners the Town was fortunate to reach satisfactory settlements without going to
court, and without occasioning any delay in the start of construction.
Throughout the year the Conservation Commission was active in acquiring land
for its purposes. Where contribution by the Commonwealth is involved, the Town Coun-
Town Counsel
sel must certify the title to land. Much work wasentailed here and, even now, is still
Robert J. Larkin
in process.
From time to time the Zoning Board of Appeals sought Mr. Larkin's advice and counsel with respect to legal
points raised in matters coming before it. The same can be said of many of the other municipal departments, especially
the Board of Selectmen, whose regular meetings the Town Counsel generally attends. At a second special Town Meet-
ing which was held during the year, it was voted to establish a Housing Authority, to amend existing Town By-lawsand
authorize the Selectmen to adopt amendments to the Town's traffic regulations. This action required some study and
research both prior and subsequent to the meeting. During the year, one law suit against the Town by a hospital, was
settled. One claim for damages caused by a defect in a highway wasrejected when it was determined that the highway
was State, not Town, controlled. At the present time there are no other claims or law suits pending against the Town.

===PAGE 11===
Town Clerk, Tax Collector, Treasurer
A Need for Realism
In many a quiet little Municipality a metamorphosis occurred in the twenty years
following World War II, with quaint little towns changed to sprawling, crowded commu-
nities, the amenities for which people originally sought the peacefulness of these
suburbs were suddenly lost as the towns increased in population.
Although during this period, the influx in our Town didnot bringabout such a vast
degree of change, a population of 2657 in 1945 and 7320 in 1965 represented close to
a 300% increase and made ours one of the most rapidly growing towns in the Common-
wealth and the fastest growing Town in Worcester County.
Many communities have handled extraordinary growth well, retaining their basic
character while accommodating great increases in population and Lunenburg, byand
large, has been such a community.
Effective tools used in Lunenburg's preservation have been the early inception of
Mary L. Padula, Town Clerk,
an active, well informed Planning Board, Sub-division Control laws, Zoning By-laws,an
Tax Collector, Treasurer.
energetic Conservation Commission, strict enforcement of sanitary rules and regulations, and the early acquisition of
up-to-date assessing methods and equipment including maps, reflecting individual property lines, and photostatic cop-
ies of deeds.
If Lunenburg is to remain on the list of well preserved, progressive communities we must continue to take realistic
steps to meet the challenge of change. One such step has.been the inception, in 1965, of a Development and I ndustrial
Commission ,for acquisition of the right type of light industry, which is geared to the needs and values of our Community,
is required if we are to broaden our tax base to sufficiently accommodate the number and quality of Municipal services
which our Community deserves and demands.
There is no golden road to efficient, economical and completely satisfactory municipal government, and finance
is forever at the heart of municipal administration as the many activities involved can only be carried out by raising
and spending large sums of money. People everywhere complain of heavy taxes, but it is likewise true that people
everywhere are demanding more and better municipal services. Holdinga budget in line becomes indeed a challenging
task. Perfection is always a desirable goal, but all too often the citizens and officials alike strive for Utopia with
little regard to where the money is coming from. Now, more than ever before, there isa need for realism and common-
sense planning in local Government. A well run government, like an individual, must gear its expenditures to its
ability to pay.
More and more local communities are clamoring for "Federal Funds" to raise themout of their budgeting doldrums.
Where, but from you andme, do these Federal funds come from and what of the Federal Government's own fiscal failings ?
Assurances that continued Federal deficits and relatively unchecked inflation will not work to the detriment of our
Country do little to reassure. A household berun on a deficit so long, ascana business or a Municipality.
Why, then, is the concept of a Federal balance of income and expenditure out of stepwith the times? Britain may have
had to publicly announce that it was devaluating its currency but our country has done no less, although the govern-
ment has been careful to call it by other names.
Municipal officials particularly should be painfully aware of the reduced buying power of the dollar. Of equal
concern are the socialistic overtones of an increasing number of our local and Federal programs. In school we were
taught that we were in competition with socialistic and communistic economic systems. It hasbeen written that "Sharing
the Wealth" cannot work without strong government control (look about you at the Federal Government's creeping in-
trusion in local and personal affairs) otherwise you have the indigent supported by the industrious, and in time even the
personal pride of honest industry will fade as the industriousrealize they are being exploited. We must have more money
for our cities and towns no matter their size but we must also have more municipal executives speaking as concerned
citizens rather than politicians for it becomesmore and more obvious that "the economic carpet upon which our Nation
has been comfortably trodding is being laid over quicksand."

Dog Officer
Love Me, Love my Dog
Nineteen hundred sixty seven proved to be an exceptionally busy year for the Dog Constable, as forty-six
dog bites, twice the 1966 number, were reported. There is no doubt that many more bites occurred which were not
reported, because of family ties or friendships. This situation is of grave concern to the Dog Officer and Health officials
as every time a dog bite occurs, regardless of ownership, it is important that the dog be restrained and closely watched
to assure that he is not rabid. Although rabies occurs very seldom, for the first time in over twenty years, two cases were
reported in Massachusetts. As rabies is almost always fatal, we once more urge that EVERY DOG BITE BE REPORTED!
Seventy-six stray dogs were picked up in 1967 as compared to forty-six the previous year. Of this number
twenty-two were unclaimed and after fifteen days were picked up by the Harvard Medical School to be used for research
purposes. Residents should assure the safety of their dogs by keeping them properly collared and licensed. More and
more complaints are being received regarding dogs running in packs. This can be a very dangerous situation.
New equipment costing $1300 is being requested at the 1968 Annual Meeting. The sum of $440 is for the purchase
of a tranquilizing gun and related equipment, which can also be used by other Town Departments. Similar equipment
is already being used in many of the surrounding communit and by various State Agencies and is reported to be effective
in the humane handling of animals. Also requested is $860 for a shortwave radio to be placed in the Dog Officer's car.
There were over 2200 telephone calls regarding dogs in 1967, many of which were first received on the police and
fire phones and several of which were of an emergency nature. A car radio would put the Dog Officer in immediate
communication with the police and fire dispatcher anytime that he is on the road.
Personnel Board
The Pressures of Inflation
The pressure of inflation coupled with a national rise in wages hashad its effect on local salaries. The Lunenburg
Personnel Board of necessity must be aware of these continuing forces upon the Town's salary structure. Local wages,
both industrial and municipal, have exhibited the results of these pressures. For example, we have witnessed great
changes in nursing salaries throughout the area in a period of but one year. The recognition of these pressures, together
with a consciousness of Town costs and subsequent effect on our taxes, influences decisions of the Board.
The various Town departments are
doing a commendable job. We are for-
tunate in having dedicated employees.
The restructuring of the wage scale is not
made on the theory that the "squeeky
wheel gets the grease" but rather on sound
arguments advocating raises on the theory
that the present schedule does not truly
reflect a fair wage.
It is the losophy of the Personnel
Board to continually seek a fair wage for
every employee of the Town of Lunenburg.
This is a continuing process of evaluation.
Recommended salaries are set by the Board
only after careful and considered judge-

Police Department
Implied Consent
Highway safety and traffic control, a major function of the Police Department,
continues to require more time than can be allotted and improper posting of traffic signs
and street legends continue to hamper effective prosecution of motor vehicle violations.
Police Chief Sherwood G. Winchester has recommended that someone be appointed to
assume the responsibility for administering the Traffic Signs and Devices Budget and for
taking the necessary steps to legalize existing signs and devicesand for the proper instal-
lation of any new ones.
The statistical report givenbelow lists operating under the influence of alcohol as
one of the motor vehicle offenses which occurs most frequently and shows that 32 arrests
for this reason were made during 1967. A recent enactment by the State Legislature re-
ferred to as the "Implied Consent" law calls for a sobriety test administered through the
Police Chief
use of a "Breatholizer". Although the new lawmakes anyone refusing to take the test
Sherwood G. Winchester
subject to a 90 day license suspension it alsohas the effect of prohibitingany convictions
for driving under the influence unless such a test has been given. The State is planning
programs to train State and Local Police in the use of the Breatholizer and the 1968 Town
Meeting will be asked to appropriate funds for the purchase of this special equipment.
Street lighting improvements throughout the Town have already helped to reduce
accidents and further improvements are planned. Parking and traffic problems which
have resulted from increased commercial development in the center of Town have been
considered at length by this Department, the Selectmenand representatives of the State
Department of Public Works, and it is hoped that this situation will be corrected in the
near future.
It has become in-
creasingly difficult to find
Sergeant, Frederick B. Hobbs

Fire Department
Hazards and Hardships
In calling for the recognition of a need for a limited number of full-time firefighters
to be employed on a day-time basis, Fire Chief James P. Deming described the scarcity
of qualified firemen during the day-time hours a matter of great concern and the most
pressing problem confronting his department. Chief Deming also praised the quality of
service rendered by the volunteer fire force and cited their wil lingness and dedication.
As the public continues to demand a higher level of fire protection, more and more
training and experience is required of our firefighters. A firefighter must understand the
chemistry of fire, the theory of ventilation, and the principle and use of breathing
apparatus. He must have a thorough knowledge of rescue work, first aid, ladder work
and hose layouts and must know how to operate portable pumps and generators. He must
know how to use and understand the function of a multiplicity of firefighting equipment,
not least of which are the 91 different items which may be found on a single fire truck.
Needless to say, a firefighter must participate in countless hours of drill work
in order to attain the required degree of proficiency and it should be pointed out that no
compensation is received for the time involved. On December 10, 1967 the hazards
Fire Chief, James P. Deming
and hardships which can be encountered by a firefighter were brought sharply into
focus with the loss of James V. Belliveau, a Fitchburg firefighter who was trapped in a burning Iding. Smoke inha-
lation can also cause varying degrees of disability and danger from falling debris and explosive materials is ever present
in a burning building. Statistics show that the most serious fires usually occur during bitter cold weather. The bitter
cold and extreme heat are two of a firefighter's worst enemies. That a fire is imore than likely to interrupt his sleep,
his family and social life or even his employment has failed to dampen the spirits of our volunteers as they remain
ready and willing to serve.
In view of the above, and the fact that the payroll for all the call men is only a little more than the salary of
one full-time firefighter, the Fire Chief is strongly recommending that the pay rate of our call firemen be increased
and a provision for such increase has been included in his 1968 budget. Also requested are appropriations for the
purchase of hose, the updating of radio equipment, and new roofing for the Fire Station. The Chief also recommends
that land adjacent to the Fire Station be purchased.
During 1967 the Fire Department answered 185 calls, a decrease of 71 from 1966. Although above normal precip-
itation during the last half of the year accounted for a substantial cut in the number of brush and grass fires, a slight
increase was noted in accident, service, cooking fires and mutual aid calls. A breakdown of calls follows: service
calls-56; brush or grass-40; accidents-16; car or truck-16; building-11; Town Dump-11; resuscitator calls-9; mutual
aid-7; cooking-5; needless-4; false-3; electrical-2; chimney-2; clothes dryer-1; drowning-1 and rescue-1.

Civil Defense
Knowledge versus Unreasonable Fear
There are many things which we would rather not
think about. At any time a nuclear bomb might fall in
our immediate vicinity. It is past the thinking stage
and we must plan.
We can no longer ignore the threat of nuclear
attack. Certainly the issue is distasteful but with a
small effort preparations can be made for fallout survival.
Practical facts about radiation and fallout are easily
accessible but civil defense warnings are to no avail as
public apathy mounts dangerously. It is time that know-
ledge replaced unreasonable fear. Pressure must be
applied in a pressing matter and efforts to educate must
be increased.
Not many realize that simple precautions could
save lives. Cellars should be stocked with water.and
non-perishable food, first aid supplies and a transistor
radio. To store a shovel, an axe, a kerosene lamp and
other camping gear, is common sense.
And there are other, more ordinary disasters
Civil Defense Director and Staff: Richard A. Brockelman, Rudolph
impending. How many families in Chicago, marooned
Masella, Richard J. Carey, James F. Plunkett, Floyd F. Barney.
in their homes by blizzards, wish they had stocked up
with Civil Defense's list of survival equipment while they waited for trucks to get through with food and other supplies?
The Civil Defense program would have been valuable in Rhode Island during the hurricane of 1938 or in Worcester after
the tornado of 1953.
Are you prepared for a natural disaster or nuclear holocaust? If not, see your local Civil Defense Director NOW!
Auxiliary Police
Support Team
We are all aware of some of the definitions of the word "auxiliary", such as giving support, helping, aiding,
assisting and used as a reserve. An emergency situation would require all of the above and then some. In Civil Defense
emergencies the movement to shelters, protection of life and property, preservation of order and prevention of crime
would be a major responsibility of your local police and its auxiliary.
Be it a catastrophe or general assistance to our Town, your Auxiliary Police stand ready to answer the call for
support. During the past year the members of the Auxiliary Police contributed over 1,050 manhours of service. This
represented a monetary savings of over $2,600 to our Town.
The following make up the membership of the Auxiliary Police Force: Captain Edward L. Worthley, Lt. Leo A.
Gagnon, Lt. Antonio L. Latini, Sgt Arthur E. Crowley, Sgt John D. Lynch, Officer Michael A. Bottalico, Officer
Earle A. Brown, Officer Walter W. Minkinen, Officer Mendall W. Coburn, Officer Frank A. Cordio, Officer Albert
Croteau, Officer Rufus B. Marvin, Officer Philip H. Peters and Officer Arthur W. Wilder.

Plumbing & Gas Permits
One Hundred Dollar Fine
Of the 84 plumbing permits issued during 1967, 21 were for new homes, 47 for homes to be remodeled and 16 for
commercial buildings. Of 95 gas permits issued, 8 were for new homes, 74 for various types of heating and cooking
appliances and 13 for commercial buildings. One gas violation was brought before the Gas Regulatory Board.
Residents of Lunenburg are reminded that plumbing or anything per-
taining to gas fitting shall not be installed, altered, removed, replaced
or repaired until a permit has been issued and that a separate permit is
required for each building. Only licensed plumbers or licensed fit-
ters are eligible to receive permits.
The new State Uniform Plumbing Code prescribes that any person
desiring to use a mobile home as a permanent residence shall obtain a
permit from the local Board of Health which Board shall, before occupancy
is permitted, make an inspection and determine that all of the provisions
of the Uniform Plumbing Code asrequired are met. Although Lunenburg's
Zoning By-law prohibits the use of a mobile home or automotive type
trailer for dwelling purposes unless located ina trailer park or permitted
Plumbing and Gas Fitting Inspector
by Board of Appeals as a replacement or as being incidental to construc-
Thomas E. Mortis
tion on or development of the premises, this act should be of particular
interest to all those persons (including park owners) locating a mobile home in an existing trailer park or other author-
ized location. Violation of anyprovision of the State Plumbing Code is punishable by a fine of up to one hundred dollars.
Tree Warden
And There Will Be Shade
The ravaging Dutch Elm disease has again taken its toll of the Town's elm trees. Lunenburg lost 139 elms this
year, the infected trees having been cut down and destroyed by burning.
The Department of Natural Resources gave a week's work, amounting to $650.00, to assist in the removal of
diseased trees. Approximately 56 samples were taken by the State and sent to the Shade Tree Laboratory, University
of Massachusetts, for culture.
Seventeen Norway Maples were planted along our roadsides, continuing the program of replacing shade trees
which had to be cut down for various reasons mostly related to highway safety. In 1967 a total of $5,956.07 was spent
on the tree program and for the control of Dutch Elm Disease, $177.22 of which amount was reimbursed by the County.
The members of the Conservation Commission and the Town
Forest Committee were deeply saddened by the death of their
N
member, Charles L. Porter, who died December 31, 1967.
Charlie was Vice Chairman of the Conservation Commission
M
and a member since the Commission was established in 1961.

always did more than his share and always refused to accept
M
any credit.

and future of the Lunenburg Conservation program.
This event dulls the enthusiasm of the Commission's report
which was written before his death. His memory will always
M

Conservation
The Time Is Now
In 1967 the voters of Lunen-
burg demonstrated a strong aware-
ness of the necessity for and many
benefits from Conservation by so-
lidly endorsing the program pre-
sented to the Town Meeting.
Conservation is the champion
of pure air, pure water, beautifi-
cation and open spaces. It is the
protector of natural resources upon
which rests the survival of the
human race. Leaders in this field
advocate that twenty-five percent
of the area of a town should be under
control of a conservation program.
The Lunenburg Conservation Com-
mission recommends that at least
twenty percent of the area of Lun-
enburg or about four thousand acres
be controlled by such a program.
Lunenburg now has about one
thousand acres under supervision
Front Row, left to right: Miss Minnie M. Martin, Mrs. William R. Peabody, Mr. Calvin
Sholl, and Mrs. Peabody's Daughter, Dorothy E. Millington. Back Row, left to right:
of the Conservation and Town Forest
Members Willis C. Woodruff, H. Benjamin Normand, William J. Deming, Jr., and
Committees, or about one fourth of
Harold D. Harley.
the recommended total. Although
an average of about one hundred acres has been added annually, at this rate, it would take thirty years to acquire the
recommended area and in the meantime much of this conservation land will have been swallowed up for various other
purposes. Needless to say, some kind of a crash program will be required.
A few persons have expressed concern that the Town will lose money by taking upany amount of otherwise buildable
land for conservation purposes. As the average new home is not "self-supporting" this is not the case. In any event,
the greater part of the land that the Commission proposes to set aside for conservation is non-buildable and non-agri-
cultural. The amount of buildable land involved will in no way hinder or prevent normal, healthy growthas there will
still remain sufficient area to permit the approximately 28,000 maximum population figure which is indicated by our
Zoning Code. On the other hand, a well thought out conservation plan involving the deve lopment and beautification
of open spaces throughout our Town and the protection of our natural resources will have a favorable influence on the
quality of our growth and protect and upgrade the property of those persons who already reside here.
Credit long overdue should be given to the ollowing outside organizations that have helped build up the Lunenburg
conservation program: The Massachusetts Department of Natural Resources, The Massachusetts Association of Conservation
Commissions, The Massachusetts Forest and Park Association, and the Massachusetts Audubon Society.
The outstanding event of the year was the bus excursions to the Cowdrey Nature Center during the "Old Home
Days" celebration sponsored by the Lunenburg Exchange Club. Over three hundred people took advantage of the trips and
hiked over the trails. This is a very popular year-round area. It is hoped to have others like it in other parts of the Town.
Once again we salute Irving Kimball, our 84-years-young consultant, who carries on anall-summer-long beauti-
fication program at the Cowdrey Nature Center. His physical feats with a hand saw are unbelievable. We also salute
our 86-years-young member of the Town Forest Committee, Mrs. William R. Peabody, who is a wonderful hostessat all
our meetings and an inspiration to the conservation cause.

Planning Board
With 1990 In Mind
The Planning Board has lost
two of its most dedicated members
and the wise counsel of Arthur E.
Colvin and Herbert H. Hoffman will
be missed. The new Board members
Messrs. Paul J. Stone and C. Kim-
ball Francis have already made
noteworthy contributions.
Mr. Stanley Styrna has been
appointed to the Sewerage Study
Committee. A report of that com-
mittee is found on Page 62 of this
report. The Planning Board itself
has been active in matters pertain-
ing to sewerage. The 1967 Town
Meeting authorized the purchase
of a parcel of land as a prospective
site for a sewerage plant and the
purchase has been made. A request
has been made for interest-free Fed-
eral Funds to be used for an engi-
neering study of a sewerage system
for the Town of Lunenburg. These
funds will not have to be paid back
until and unless the study is effected.
L to R: Paul J. Stone, William A. Mason, Jr., Stanley Styrna, Harold D. Harley and
C. Kimball Francis.
Mr. William A. Mason was elected to represent the local Board on the Montachusett Regional Planning Commission.
In 1967, Mr. Mason attended several meetings and believes that, although the Commission has been slow in organizing,
it will play an important part in our area planning.
The subdivisions "Whalom Estates" and "Windward Terrace" were approved by the Board in 1967 and several
others are currently under review. It is fortunate that throughout the years the Board has adhered rigidly to all rules
and regulations concerning subdivision, otherwise the Town would presently be burdened by expensive street repairs
and similar inconveniences.
The matter of removing loam and sand was actively studied. The services of Board Consultant, Samuel Snow, in
this matter were extensive. Unless restrictions are placed on soil removal, some areas of this Town will become obsolete
and worthless. The Board recognizes that sand and loam removal is essential, but believes that after the land is stripped
it can be economically restored for future productive use.
The Town Dump is becoming a problem. Space is limited and access to the area is hazardous to vehicles. The
Board has inspected adjacent land and has drawn up a plan which would meet the future dumping needs of the Town.
The proposed plan calls for a paved road to the dumping area.
The Planning Board meets the first and third Wednesdays of every month at the Brooks House. Anyone wishing to
attend one of these meetings should contact the Chairman, Mr. William A. Mason.

===PAGE 53===
Development & Industrial Commission
To meet The Change
The most important accomplishment of this Commission in 1967 was to lay the groundwork for the Special Town
Meeting on June 28th, which meeting voted to change the zoning at the south end of Lancaster Avenue to "commercial".
This was in anticipation of a large and respected company buying the land and erecting a building on it. To date, this
has not developed, but there is still interest in the property by the company.
This rezoning may seem to have been done prematurely by those people not aware of the thinking and passion for
secrecy of some corporations. However, the Commission is unanimous in agreeing that it must be done this way if we
hope to enticed desirable firm to ourared. With this thought in mind, the Industrial Development Commission is preparing
to ask the town to rezone other parcels of land in the near future in anticipation of other corporate ventures.
The five hundred dollars asked for and voted by the Special Town Meeting was not used and has been returned
to the general treasury.
The Commission continues to note sporadic interest in Lunenburg by a variety of firms and educational institutions.
It has faithfully and diligently tracked them all down to its satisfaction, but it still has to make its first "hit".
The latest gimmick on the scene is the Massachusetts Industrial Development Financing Authority, which makes
it possible for the first time in this State for local citizens, duly organized, to offer bond financing to prospective new
corporate taxpayers. The Commission has decided to wait and see how this develops, before asking the Town to vote
such an Authority into existence.
Industrial Development Commission: Mendall W. Colburn, John E. Rafferty, William A. Allen, 2nd, Andrew M. Bonnallie,
Rodney A. Karlson, Norman C. Cross. Absent when picture was taken - Almon O. Ruggles.

Inspector of Weights & Measures
Accuracy Cited
Scales, gasoline pumps, fuel oil truck meters and other commercially used weigh-
ing and measuring devices are regularly tested for accuracy once a year and unannounced
spot checks are frequently made.
Of the 156 weighing devices inspected in 1967, 151 were found to be correct
and sealed and 3 pumps and 2 scales were condemned. Lunenburg businessmen are to be
commended for their accuracy in this regard. It is rarely that inaccuracies are found.
The following legislation enacted by the 1967 Legislature affords further protec-
tion to the consumer:
Chapter 19. An act requiring that computing scales be maintained at all retail
outlets, engaged in the business of selling pre-packaged meats, poultry or edible fish
at retail, and requiring that pre-packaged items of meat, etc., be reweighed in the
presence of the prospective buyer when so requested, providing a penalty of a fine of
not less than $100.00 for violation.
Chapter 92. An act providing greater protection from fraud and deceit in the
purchase of heating oils by requiring additional data on certificates required to be issued
Raymond F. Butterfield
to the purchaser whenever ten or more gallons are delivered.
Inspector of Weights
and Measures.
In 1967 an act was also passed which provides for Certificates of Peddlers Applications by the Chief of Police
rather than by the Board of Selectmen.
In accordance with provisions of Section
41, Chapter 98 of the General Laws of Massach-
usetts, all inhabitants or persons having usual
places of business within the Town using weighing
0
or selling goods, wares or merchandise, for public
weighing or for hire or reward, are hereby noti-
fied to bring them in to be retested, adjusted
and sealed.
The Inspector of Weights and Measures
extends his appreciation to the various establish-
ments in the Town of Lunenburg for the coopera-
tion and assistance rendered.

Library
Between the Pages
Long to be remembered and overshadowing all other events of the year, not only for the library staffand its
patrons but for all her many friends in the Town of Lunenburg, was the death of Miss Edith K. Proctor, dedicated chief
librarian of Ritter Memorial Library from 1932 until the time of her death on April 10, 1967. The adult reading room in
which she spent many hours of her life was dedicated to her on December 3, 1967. The bronze plaque reads: "This
room is dedicated to the memory of Edith K. Proctor, Librarian 1932-1967, by her friends, relatives, the Lunenburg
Memorial Fund, and the trustees of the library." Two round tables and eight chairs given by the Memorial Fund and
a card file given by Harold and Margaret Proctor Harley, in memory of Edith K. Proctor, her mother, Mrs. Lizzie
Kimball Francis Proctor, and their dear friend, Willa J. Harley," complete the furnishings.
Mrs. Eleanor B. Whitcomb, children's librarian for several years, ably assumed the duties of chief librarian with
Mrs. Ruth M. Proctor acting asher assistant. Mrs. Whitcomb's resignation was accepted with regret on August 31, 1967.
The position is now held by Miss Liana G. Kolb who has
taught for several years in our school system and has had
library experience. Several students have been added
to the staff as part-time pages and desk assistants.
A Dennison copier was purchased for the library with
funds ibuted by Mr. George R. Wallace, 3rd, and from
the Library Equipment Fund. This equipment for reproducing
material in the library's collection or otherwise facilitating
study and research has been used extensively and proven to
be of great assistance and convenience to our patrons.
Another addition to the library's services has been the
loan to patrons of books, films, and reproduct from perio-
dicals available through the Interlibrary Loan Service of the
Central Massachusetts Regional Library System.
A pre-school children's story hour is held on the first
and third Friday mornings of each month from 10:30 to 11:00.
Mrs. Lorraine Howe, an experienced nursery and kindergar-
ten teacher, has captivated the children with her colorful
and varied interpretations of new and old story favorites.

The first year of the Medicare program went welland the Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service was recerti-

persons under twenty-one and over sixty-five years of age and has accounted for a drop

their pre-schoolers to their own doctor.
New State laws, which became effective in December, 1967, require every school
child, kindergarten through grade eight to be immunized against Small Pox, Diphtheria,
Tetanus, Pertussis (Whooping Cough), Measles and Polio unless exempted for medical
or religious reasons.

age who attended.

Nashoba Associated Boards of Health
A Combined Effort
Regionalization, the latest trend in Governmental Services, is not new to the Nashoba Associated Boards of
Health which organization has been offering health services on a regional basis since 1931.
Henry T. Smith, who for many years served as Sanitation Officer, continued as acting Medical Director and
Donald Melloon was promoted to Senior Sanitation Officer. The position of Medical Social Consultant was changed
from a four day week to full-time to accommodate the new Medicare and Medicaid programs. A Nutritionist, Dental
Hygienist and Dental and Medical Consultants also serve on the Nashoba team.
The Dental Hygienist and Consultants made a special effort to motivate parents in securing better dental care
for their children. First grade and fifth grade children were examined for dental defects and second and eighth graders
received examinations, cleaning and topical fluoride applications Dental Health Educational programs were conducted
in the first and fourth grade classrooms. Of the 452 Lunenburg school children participating in the Dental programs
218 or 47% were referred to their dentists. Fifteen Well Child Conferences, at which 142 children made 218 Clinic
visits, were held. Tine tests for Tuberculosis were administered to 51 school personnel and 286 children in grades
one to nine. Immunizations were given in the school and at the Well Child Conferences. A rabies immunization Clinic
for dogs was conducted.

Welfare Department
A Major Move
The local Welfare Office is located in the Town Hall. The money used to finance
the Welfare programs is derived from three sources, Federal and State Government grants
and from local taxation assessed to the citizens of Lunenburg. Funds for the Town's share
of the programs are appropriated at the Annual Town Meeting.
The programs administered by this department are Medical Assistance or "Medicaid",
General Relief, Aid to Families with Dependent Children, Disability Assistance and Old
Age Assistance. Because all of these programsare a combination of Federal, State and
local responsibility they are based on Federal and State Laws and must be administered
according to these laws,
Among the most serious problems confronting many individuals and families today is
Welfare Agent,
the continued rise in the cost of medical care. Tohelpmeet this situation The Massachusetts
Gladys M. Goodale
Department of Public Welfare on September 1, 1966, put into effect an expanded programof medical assistance to give
comprehensive medical care to residents who do not have enough money to provide for their own medical needs.
This is the program called "Medicaid" and is jointly supported by your Federal, State and Town monies.
During the past year the number of persons receiving assistance on the "Medicaid" program has substantially in-
creased. Listed below is an illustration of the effect of this new medical program.
January
1967
66 persons
$ 5,167.55
April
1967
91 persons
7,009.33
October
1967
163 persons
11,007.13
December 1967
177 persons
9,661.50
Medical Assistance is available to persons under 21 years of age who do not have sufficient income and/or
resources to meet the cost of medical care; persons over 65 years with income and/or resources which are sufficient for
their general living expenses but are insufficient to meet the cost of medical care; persons permanently and totally
disabled with income and/or resources which are sufficient for their general living expenses but are insufficient to meet
the cost of medical needs; parents who would be otherwise eligible for an AFDC grant but do not meet residence,
excess income and personal property requirements.
Eligibility for the program depends on income, savings and other resources and the local Welfare Office should
be contacted if more information is desired regarding this program.
The recent enactment of Chapter 658 of the Acts of 1967 changes the local Public Welfare function to State
operation control, effective July 1, 1968. The State Department in Boston has committees studying changes in the
policies and in the administration of the programs and it is believed that an orderly transfer from Town to State will
take place on July 1, 1968.

Veterans Services
They Have Served
The need for Veterans' Services continues to increase.
In 1967 20 families were
extended Veterans' Benefits and $14,434.47 or $6,934.77 more than the amount appropr-
liated, was paid out. The functions of this department are subject to State and Federal rules
and regulations and each case must be carefully investigated before authorization of payment
is requested from the State Department of Veterans' Services. If a case fails to qualify, the
Town does not receive the 50% reimbursement which is otherwise forthcoming from the State.
A variety of Veterans' Services were made available to an additional 42 veterans,
including training and loans under the G. 1. Bill, disability compensations, widow's pensions
and hospital benefits.
Veterans' Agent, Edward H. Strickland, has stressed the importance of qualified
veterans availing themselves of the facilities of the Veterans' Administration Hospital in
the area. Either this office or a family doctor is willing and able to advise any veteran of
the proper administrative procedure. The ever increasing cost of hospitalization and drugs
accounted for a large portion of this year's budget overdraft. The long term illness of five
veterans during the past year also made it necessary to request additional funds.
Agent ,Edward H. Strickland
CEMETERY RULES AND REGULATIONS
as amended November 28, 1966
1. All burials and removals shall be conducted under the supervision of the Superintendent.
2. No interments shall be made except in a concrete vault.
3. No trees shall be planted and only such shrubs may be planted as are approved by the Commission. The planting of such shrubs
must be done under the supervision of the Superintendent.
4. Charges for burials shall be as follows:
Regular burials
$ 40.00
(From December first to April first and holidays.)
75.00
Stillborn
10.00
Crematory remains
10.00
The Commissioners reserve the right to make a charge, in addition to the above schedule, in any case where it becomes necessary.
5. Perpetual Care shall consist of the following: a. Raking and removing dead leaves and grass. b. Periodic mowing of lots, when
reasonably necessary. C. Filling sunken graves.
6. Any individual having a right or rights of burial in any lot shall, by paying a sum of money, as hereinafter stipulated, provide
for Perpetual Care.
7. The charges for Perpetual Care shall be as follows:
] to 3 grave lot
$ 75.00
4 to 6 grave lot
100.00
7 to 9 grave lot
150.00
10 to 12 grave lot
200.00
8. All individuals requesting a grave lot, or before exercising a right of burial in a lot shall, before a grave is opened, fill out and
sign, in duplicate, an application therefor and submit the same to the Superintendent. Such forms shall be entitled "Cemetery Appli-
cation" and shall be in such formas the Cemetery Commissioners deem necessary. The same may be obtained from the Commissioners
or the Superintendent.
9. All money due on a grave lot shall be paid before opening a grave unless satisfactory arrangements have been made with the
Cemetery Commissioners for their payment.
10. In the event of a default in the payment of agreed charges, the Commissioners reserve the right to reassign any unoccupied grave lot.
11. Sums of money, in addition to the amounts otherwise specified herein, may be accepted, at the discretion of the Commissioners,
for special care.
12. No monument allowed on less than a two grave lot, only a market on a one grave lot.
13. All fees due the Cemetery Department must be paid before setting of stones.

Highways
A Clean Sweep
Many and varied services and activities are performed by the Highway Depart-
ment. Following is an explanation of what and where work was accomplished during
1967 on the Town's 76.37 miles of public roads,
Chapter 81 - Approximately 19 miles of bituminous surface were treated with 56,615
gallons of asphalt and 2,389 yards of sand forcover. A drainage program costing $2,966
consisted of the following: a drainage ditch was dug on Cross Road and a basin installed;
on Hollis Road and West Street catch basins and cross culverts were installed to elimi-
nate flooding conditions. Other drainage projects were completed on Main Street,
Leominster Road, Goodrich Street, Northfield Road, Howard Street, New West Town-
send Road and Summer Street. Catch basins all over the Town were cleaned. Approxi-
mately 212 tons of patch were used to repair holes in the roads. The sum of $234 was
spent on our yearly program of roadside mowing which helps to eliminate roadside brush.
Four tons of calcium chloride were applied to Round Road, Goodrich Street, and Leom-
inster Road. Guard rail was replaced on Electric Avenue, and a bridge temporarily
repaired on Flat Hill Road. The addition of a Wayne Sweeper greatly facilitated the
sweeping program. Although the sweeper was delivered June 1st, too late for the early
Supt. Streets Richard W.Brown
spring cleaning, approximately ten times the usual number of roads were swept, and
the sweeper was used extensively in the oiling program. This piece of equipment also permits one man to do the work
of many.
General Highway Maintenance - Patching was done on various roads at a cost of $2,127.92. The widening project
on Goodrich Street commenced in 1965 has been completed; Kilburn Street was also widened. Gravel and gravelling
cost $1,653. Spring cleaning and sweeping, which entails removing excess sand from the sides of various streets ,cost
approximately $547. Round Road, Reservoir Road, Gibson, Goodrich and Kilburn Streets were scraped. Patching
frost heaves on various roads cost $446.60. Drainage pipe was installed on West Street for distance of approximately
300 feet. Drainage projects were also taken care of on Page, Main, Holman, Whiting Streets, Lancaster and Oak
Avenues, and Flat Hill and Northfield Roads, at a total cost of $4,632.61. Calcium chloride was applied on various
roads at a cost of $236.90. Extensive roadside mowing was also performed.
Chapter 90 Maintenance - Surface treatment of Lancaster Avenue has been carried out in stages over a period of several
years. This year's work completed the project. The intersection of Fort Pond Road and Shirley Road wasalso given the
same type of surfacing.
Chapter 90 Construction - Bituminous concrete Type 1, better known as hot-top,was applied on a section of Shirley-
Leominster Road from the Leominster line to approximately the intersection of Lancaster Avenue. Work on the Shirley-
Leominster Road will be continued next year. The long overdue widening and reconstruction of Leominster Road was
commenced for a distance of approximately 2, 100 feet from the Leominster line at d cost of approximately $30,000.
Leominster Road will eventually be reconstructed to the Center of Town.
Road Oil and Tar - The following roads were treated with 23,593 gallons of asphalt, at a cost of $4,000: Burrage,
Howard, Goodrich, Prospect, Kilburn, West and Pleasant Streets and Northfield Road.
Snow Removal - Due to the early snow storms in November and the exceptional cold which caused severe icing con-
ditions, more salt and sand were used this year than normally. The total cost of snow removal was $43,122.92.
Road Machinery Maintenance This account encompasses the maintenance and upkeep of all our equipment. As
a
Highway Department's efficiency is greatly influenced by the condition of its equipment, the equipment repairman must
see that all vehicles are in good repair at all times. The Highway operators are instructed to report any mechanical
difficulty immediately, as preventive maintenance is their watch word. A mechanical hydraulic jack was purchased
this year to handle transmission and clutch repairs. This purchase will result in asavings to the Town, as formerly such
repairs were done elsewhere. Gas, oil, grease, diesel fuel, acetylene, oxygen, grader and plow blades, chains and
tires were purchased out of this account.
- 58 -
===PAGE 61===
Special Accounts - Continuing our program of replacing old and damaged street signs, 36 signs which are visible at
night were purchased. The poison ivy spraying program was renewed this year and extensive brushcutting was accom-
plished under a special article. The program of extending sidewalks was continued with a new section added to the
Whalom Road sidewalk which will be completed in 1968. Extensive drainage was installed on Howard Street for a
distance of 056 feet. A drainage ditch and two catch basins were installed on Brown Avenue to pick up surface
water. A Chevrolet truck and plow, portable welder and Wayne Sweeper were purchased.
Recommendations:
1. Purchase of a Grader.
Our present grader, a 1946 model, is costing more to repair than it is worth. A grader is one of our most
important pieces of equipment as it is used extensively on home jobs, and for resurfacing roads and is
invaluable in cutting ice and winging back.snow drifts.
2. Purchase of a Dump Truck.
It is recommended that a new truck be purchased to replace a 1957 International, in accordance with the
program for replacing older equipment.
3. Purchase of a Roller.
It is felt that the addition of this equipment would assure smoother road surfaces and prevent rescheduling
projects because of the unavailability of a rented machine. This equipment could also be used on the
construction of sidewalks.
4. Purchase of a Sander Chassis.
This sander would replace a ten year old sander which has really "had it" and cannot be welded together
another time.
In the interest of economy, the Highway Department feels that the above equipment should be purchased this year.
Veteran Highway Department Employee, John J. Flanagan, with 1967 Addition to Highway Fleet.

Dark Department
Overwhelming Response
In 1967 more people than ever before participated in the Park Department Program. Response like thismore than
justifies the Park Department's emphasis on qualified supervision and a widely diversified program. The addition of a
maintenance supervisor resulted in improved maintenance at a reduced cost and extensive improvements were made at
the various parks and playgrounds.
New programs which were proposed and put into effect were an adult scotch foursome golf league, a juniorand
senior tennis program, junior olympic development track meets, flag football and an indoor winter tennis program.
Town Beach participation was considerably higher than in 1966 with a new tiny tot swim class added to the already
successful swim program. A total of $2,232.50 was collected from the sale of swim tickets and turned over to the Town
Treasurer.
The following recreational programs are available to the young people of Lunenburg: Supervised playground
activities at the Center and Whalom areas; morning baseball league; girl's softball; junior basketball; girl's basketball;
senior basketball; biddy basketball; tennis instruction; swimming instruction; soap box derby; field day (track and field
events with a cookout); trips to points of interest; flag football; junior olympic development; winter gym periods and
skating.
Adult recreational programs include a softball,league, men's golf
league, scotch foursome league, indoor and outdoor tennis, swimming,
skating and an open gym period during the winter.
Interior renovations to the building at the Town Beach were com-
pleted with monies appropriated at the 1967 Town Meeting. Also appro-
priated was the sum of $4,500 for the Band Shell which is pictured at the
right and which is to be located on the School grounds off Massachusetts
Avenue. The pre-fabricated Shell will be completed and ready for use
in the summer of 1968.
Recognizing the need for improved winter recreation facilities, the Park Department is proposing an outdoor
winter skiing-sliding facility and a paved skating area which can also be used as as play area during the summer months.
An article which will be included in the 1968 Town Meeting Warrant, calls for the appropriation of $3,000 to develop
this winter recreation site.
Other proposals to be submitted to the
Town Meeting are an appropriation of $500
to purchase playground equipment for the Town
Beach and for the new playground on Reservoir

lights at the Marshall Park Tennis Courts.

Sewerage Study Committee
Still Searching
The 1967 Annual Town Meeting authorized the Board of Selectmen and Moderator to appoint a Sewerage Study
Committee. In April the Committee had its organizational meeting and hasheld monthly meetings and special meetings
since that time. After much deliberation the Committee decided to first ask the property owners how they felt regarding
a Town sewer and to try to determine the extent of our sewerage problems in general. In September of 1967 a question-
naire was drafted and approved by the Committee and 2500 questionnaires were mailed to taxpayers in the Town. There
was an approximate 30% response to this survey which would seem to indicate that homeowners are interested in and
concerned about the problem. Given below is a resume of the questions asked and the answers received.
1. Have you had any problem with your present system?
3. Type of system?
YES 228
NO 574
Septic tank
683
Back-up into the house
115
Cesspool
98
Odor outside
123
Other
20
Odor inside
44
Unknown
9
Moisture on surface of drain field
130
4. How large is your lot?
2. If your system has failed, do you know why?
1/4 Acre
159
Ground water
79
1/3 Acre
75
Faulty construction
34
1/2 Acre
152
System too small
39
3/4 Acre
97
Poor soil conditions
126
1 Acre or more
295
5. Would you be willing to connect to a town sewer system if it were available to you? YES - 544
NO - 248
Although the majority of replies indicated no problems, many of these systems were new, or systems used only
seasonally. Where systems failed, 45% of the reported failures were attributable to poor soil conditions and 28% to
ground water. This substantiates a fact which many Townspeople already know, that the Town is predominately clay.
In view of the above survey and other findings the Committee concluded that a solution to our sewerage problems
can only be found by obtaining a public sewer system. Special meetings were then up to consult with and be advised
by various engineering firms who are in a position to relate information on the best way for a study committee to proceed
with planning and secure information regarding Federal and State funds which may be applied toward a public sewer
system. The Committee is of the opinion that a firm proposal should not be offered to the Townspeople at this time as the
regional concept, which is under consideration by the Planning Board and Selectmen, could afford a higher degree of
Federal and State funding than is available on an individual basis. In any event a public sewer for the Town could not
be installed within the next year or two as there is a considerable amount of groundwork to be performed before the
actual engineering is started. It is, therefore, recommended that the study committee be continued and an article
calling for its continuation has been included in the 1968 Annual Meeting Warrant.
Sewerage Study Committee: Ralph M. Bickford, Jr., Stanley Styrna, Robert D. Clarke, Martha L. Molaghan.
Absent when picture was taken: Carl G. Smith

Council for the Aging
Interest Expressed
There are today in excess of 620,000 persons in Massachusetts 65 years of age or over and this number is increasing
yearly. It has been established that approximately 12% of Lunenburg's residents are 65 years of age or over. Reverend
Leon R. Oliver and Reverend John M. O'Brien serve as Co-Chairmen of Lunenburg's Council for the Aging, which was
organized in the summer of 1966 to carry out programs designed to meet the problems of the aging.
Two surveys have been taken by the local Council. The first showed recreational, social and housing needs.
The second, a special survey on housing needs, showed 60 persons interested in low-rent apartments in Lunenburg.
The Council then investigated the means of establishing a local housing authority and on November 20, 1967 at a
Special Town Meeting called for that purpose, the Townspeople voted to organize and establish the Lunenburg Housing
Authority to provide housing for elderly persons of low income, the members of which are to be initially appointed by
the Board of Selectmen and then, with the exception of one member who is appointed by the Governor, elected by the
Town Meeting.
To be eligible for this housing a citizen must be 65 years of age with an income of less than $2,500; a couple's
income cannot exceed $3,000. Dwellings are planned for single or double occupancy and dwellings for two persons
may be occupied by a married couple, two men or two women or a brother and sister and in some cases by an elderly
tenant and a non-elderly person provided that person is necessary for the physical well being of the elderly person and
is of low income.
At the present time programs for the elderly are held in the Legion House on Wednesday afternoons from 2:00 to
4:00 P.M. and elderly citizens are invited to take part. More and more owners of various types of recreational facil-
ities and transportation companies are offering special group rates to Senior Citizens. It is hoped that the eventual attend-
ance at the Wednesday programs will warrant the planning of special excursions for Lunenburg's Senior Citizens at
special group rates.
Representing Council for the Aging, left to right: Signe Schultz, Rev. John M. O'Brien, Rosaire Chapdelaine,
Co-Chairman Rev. Leon R. Oliver, and Gladys M. Goodale.
- 63 -
===PAGE 66===
Community Action Committee
Off to d Head Start
Although the economic well being and prosperity of the United States has surpassed any achieved in world history,
poverty continues to be the lot of a substantial number of people. The United States can achieve its full economic and
social potential as a nation only if every individual has the opportunity to contribute to the full extent of his or her
capabilities, by opening to everyone the opportunity for education and training, the opportunity to work and live in
decency and dignity. With this in mind, the Economic Opportunity Act of 1964 was established. The office of the
Economic Opportunity Act directly administers such programs as, Community Action, Job Corps and Volunteers in
Service to America (VISTA). Some related programs are: Neighborhood Youth Corpsand Small Business Administration.
The Lunenburg Community Action Committee was appointed. on November 7, 1966 by the Selectmen and given
the task to study and develop programs under the Economic Opportunity Act. One such program is "Operation Headstart"
for pre-school children. The purpose of "Operation Headstart" is to improve the health and physical ability of
children, to develop their self-confidence and ability to relate to others, to increase their verbal and conceptual skills,
to involve parents in activities with their children. The first Operation Headstart" program was held during the summer
of 1966 with 26 children taking part in it at a total cost of about $8,000. The cost to our Town was approximately
$800 which was the value of providing space in our schools. A similar program for 30 children was conducted in the
summer of 1967. Application to the Economic Opportunity Act for allocation of funds for the 1968 summer program
has been submitted.
During 1967, four youths of our community worked in the Neighborhood Youth Corps.
The Fitchburg United Fund, of which Lunenburg is a member, has allocated funds for the Legal Aid program and
during 1967 there were ten cases in which aid was given to local residents. Presently under study is the possible
affiliation with the Fitchburg Community Action Committee, which is a member of the Montachusett Opportunity Council,
as changes in requirements for representation under the Economic Opportunity Act may make this desirable.
No other programs are planned at present as your committee feels that further study is necessary in this area.
The Home Department of the Extension Service met regularly during 1967. Emphasis was placed on adjusting patterns and crea-
ting original styles. Meets were generally held on the first Thursday of each month. Included in the services rendered by the Home de-
partment was a donation to All Saints Chapel and the donation of pattern books to the Library and the Junior-Senior High School.
Every organization has its ups and downs and "4-H" is exception. We were fortunate in having Margery Stillman accept the
Town Chairmanship. Marg's capable, enthusiastic leadership was what wasneeded to revitalize the local 4-H organization To see
these youngsters start at nine and grow in knowledge, abilities and interests to capable teenagers is extremely satisfying. A 4-H'r
who has participated in the various 4-H programs is more than likely to grow into responsible person and become an asset, not a pro-
blem, to the community. The many unselfish ladies who have given of their time, reaprewards when they see smiling 4-H'rs wearing
the dresses they have made or leading a heifer from the show ring as he carries away a shiny trophy.
A 4-H'r participates in many activities, not just the local club meetings. In September,4-H displays were placed in each school!
and a letter regarding the 4-H program was home with the school children. As a result over sixty children and parents attended the
first rally which consisted of singing, games and refreshments and a talk on the 4-H programs. In November, Awards Night was held,
with a pot-luck supper, a film was shown and awards were presented by Robert Smiley, our 4-H agent in Worcester County.
Our older 4-H'rs are part of the State and National programs. This year Gereth Stillman was a delegate in entomology to the
National 4-H Congress in Chicago and he also participated in the County Exchange trip to Michigan. Gereth was also chosen a member
of the State 4-H Dairy Judging Team, following in the footsteps of his brother and sister, Gregg and Suzanne. Stephen Craig received
a trophy in the Egg Laying Contest in the Poultry project. Ten members of the Wachusett Dairy Club participated in the Spring Dairy
Judging Tour. In August Gereth and Shaun Stillman showed their heifers in the State Dairy Show in Springfield. Lunenburg made a
good showing at the 4-H held at the 4-H Camp in Spencer, at which time Shaun, Gereth and Glenn Stillman won rosettes for out-
standing achievement and Shaun was presented a trophy for Intermediate Showman and Glenn a trophy for outstanding Junior Holstein
Showman. In cooperation with the local Grange Fair the 4-H committee presented Karen Bourque and Kathy Miller trophies for out-
standing dairy and sewing exhibits.

Article 15. To see if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by adding a new article to beknown as Article XVII, as follows:
Article XVII:
Section 1. There shall be a Committee called the Building Committee, which shall consist of not more
than seven (7) members, to be appointed by the Selectmen and Moderator, three (3) of whom shall be
initially appointed for a term of one (1) year, two (2) of whom shall be initially appointed for a term of
two (2) years, and two (2) of whom shall be initially appointed for a term of three (3) years. Thereafter
each member shall be appointed for a term of three years. Any vacancy in the committee shall be filled
by the Selectmen and Moderator. Subject to specific authorization by the Town, said Committee shall
have charge and direction of the construction and reconstruction of all buildingsowned, leased or occu-
pied by the Town, except that any Building Committee now in existence shall complete its prescribed
duties.
MOTION NOT CARRIED.

The agreement for the establishment of the Northern Worcester County Regional Vocational School District, nowbeing Monta-
chusett Regional Vocational Technical School District, as amended, shall be further amended by substituting the following provisions
in place of the preamble, and sub-section 1 (C):
PREAMBLE:
"This agreement is entered into pursuant to Chapter 568 of the Acts of 1964 as amended by Chapter 543 of the Acts of
1965 by and among the cities of Fitchburg and Gardner, and the towns of Ashburnham, Ashby, Barre, Harvard, Hubb-
ardston, Lunenburg, Phillipston, Royalston, Sterling, and Winchendon, Massachusetts. In consideration of the mutual
promises herein contained, it is hereby agreed as follows:"
Subsection 1 (C):
"(C) At the regular city elections in 1967 and at the regular city elections in every odd-numbered year thereafter the
cities of Fitchburg and Gardner shall each elect one member to serve on the Committee for a term of four years. At the
annual Town elections in 1968 and at the annual Town elections in every fourth year thereafter, the towns of Ashburnham,
Ashby, Barre, Harvard, Hubbardston and Lunenburg shall elect one member to serve on the Committee for a term of four
years. At the annual Town election in 1968 the towns of Phillipston, Royalston, Sterling and Winchendon shall elect
one member to serve on the Committee for a term of two years and at the annual Town elections in 1970 and at the annual
Town elections in every fourth year thereafter, the said Towns shall each elect one member to serve on the Committee for
a term of four years." VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.

- 73 -
===PAGE 76===
RAISE BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE THE SUM OF $300 FOR THE USE OF THE COUNCIL, SAID LAW TO BE AS FOLLOWS:
ARTICLE XVIII - COUNCIL ON AGING:
SECTION 1. THERE IS HEREBY ESTABLISHED A LOCAL COUNCIL ON AGING CONSISTING OF NOT LESS THEN SEVEN (7)
NOR MORE THAN ELEVEN (11) VOTING MEMBERS. OF THIS NUMBER, THE CHAIRMAN, AND NOT LESS THAN THREE (3) NOR
MORE THAN FIVE (5) PERSONS, SHALL BE APPOINTED BY THE BOARD OF SELECTMEN FROMTHE REGISTERED VOTERS OF THE
TOWN OF LUNENBURG. THE CHAIRMAN OF THE COUNCIL, WHEN APPOINTED, SHALL DESIG NATE THE REMAININ MEMBERS
OF THE COUNCIL FROM INTERESTED AND REPRESENTATIVE GROUPS IN THE COMMUNITY. THE LOCAL COUNCIL ON AGING
SHALL BE RESPONSIBLE TO THE BOARD OF SELECTMEN, AND ITS MEMBERS SHALL SERVE WITHOUT COMPENSATION, AND
WITHIN THE LIMITS OF AVAILABLE FUNDS. IT MAY APPOINT SUCH CLERKS AND OTHER EMPLOYEES AS IT MAY REQUIRE.
IT SHALL BE THE DUTY OF THE LOCAL COUNCIL ON AGING TO CARRY OUT PROGRAMS DESIGNED TO MEET THE PROBLEMS
OF THE AGING IN COORDINATION WITH PROGRAMS OF THE COMMISSION ON AGING ESTABLISHED UNDER GENERAL
LAWS, CHAPTER 6, SECTION 73, AND TO SUBMIT AN ANNUAL REPORT TO THE TOWN AND A COPY THEREOF TO SAID
COMMISSION.

or act anything thereon. (Disapproved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED TO RAISE BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE $1,000.
Article 56. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum of
$6,900 for Chapter 90 Construction on Leominster Road, Townsend Harbor Road and Prospect Street, or act anything thereon.
(Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO RAISE BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE $6,767.74 AND
TO APPROPRIATE FROM CHAPTER 90 FUNDS APPROPRIATED IN 1963 $132.26.
Article 57. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum of
$48,600, or some other sum or sums, to pay the State and County shares of Chapter 81 and 90 Highway Work inanticipation of allot-
ments from the State and County, these sums to be restored to the Surplus Revenue Account upon receipt of said allotments from the
State and County, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO RAISE BY TAX-
ATION AND APPROPRIATE $18,600 AND TO TRANSFER FROM THE SURPLUS REVENUE ACCOUNT $30,000.
Article 58. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum of
$19,300, or some other sum or sums, for the purpose of operating, repairing and purchasing road machinery, or actanything thereon.
(Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO APPROPRIATE THE SUM OF $19,300 FOR THE PURPOSE OF
OPERATING, REPAIRING AND/OR PURCHASING ROAD MACHINERY FOR THE HIGHWAY DEPARTMENT, THE SUM OF $10,500
TO BE TRANSFERRED FROM THE MACHINERY RENTAL ACCOUNT TO THE MACHINERY MAINTENANCE ACCOUNT, AND THE
BALANCE OF $8,800 TO BE RAISED BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATED.
Article 59. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Selectmen to acquire by eminent domain, purchase, or otherwise,
such land or interest in land as may be necessary for the purpose of the relocation and/or alteration of streets within the Town of
Lunenburg including Reservoir Road, and to raise by taxation and appropriate the sum of $2,000 for said purpose, or act anything
thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO RAISE BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE $2,000.
Article 60. To see if the Town will vote to transfer from the Tree Warden's Machinery Rental Account the sum of $255, or some
other sum or sums, for the purchase of a one-man Chain Saw for the Tree Department, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the
Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO TRANSFER FROM THE TREE WARDEN'S MACHINERY RENTAL ACCOUNT $255.
Article 61. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum of
$2,000, or some other sum or sums, for the purchase of a new Police Cruiser, one of the present Cruisers to be traded as part of the
consideration for the proposed new Cruiser, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee. )VOTED UNANIMOUSLY
TO RAISE BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE $2,000.
Article 62. To see if the Town will vote to accept the bequest of $1,000 to the Ritter Memorial Library under the will of the
late Margaret Dillon Fessenden. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 63. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Selectmen to sell at public auction any and all property foreclosed
up to this time and which may be foreclosed during 1967 under Tax Title proceedings and to sign, execute, and deliver in the name
of the Town, proper deeds of conveyance or release thereof, the said auction to be advertised fourteen (14) days before the sale, said
advertisement to be published for five (5) days preceding the sale, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 64. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Treasurer, with the approval of the Selectmen, to borrow money from
time to time in anticipation of the revenue for the financial years beginning January 1, 1967, and January 1, 1968, in accordance
with the provisions of General Laws, Chapter 44, Section 4, and to renew any note or notes as may be given for period of less than
one year, in accordance with the provisions of General Laws, Chapter 44, Section 17. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 65. To see if the Town will vote to choose a Director in accordance with Chapter 128 of the General Laws. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY TO CHOOSE SUE LOGAN AS DIRECTOR.
Article 66. To see if the Town will vote to accept the several sums of money for Perpetual Care of various cemetery lots,
approved by the Cemetery Commissioners, as follows: Samuel and Elizabeth Caldwell - $100; Stanley B. Fessenden - $300; Porter
H. Harley - $75; Hugo Jamnback - $75; Toivo J. Johnson - $75; Emil T. Koivisto - $75; Albert P. Mac Cracken - $100; Richard F.
Mulligan - $100; Gustav A. Rosen $75; Elwin E. Stone $75; Wilfred Valcourt $100. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.

School Building Committee
At the Special Town Meeting on January 16, 1967 the voters of Lunenburg authorized the School Building
Committee to proceed with the construction of the 700 pupil Middle School on the Fessenden property adjacent to the
Central Complex. The school is to initially house part of Grade 4 and Grades 5, 6 and 7 and is estimated to cost
approximately $1,541,500 for construction, equipment and land costs.
After approval by the Town the first task of the Building Committee was to finalize the preliminary plans of the
school so that the design would best meet the proposed educational program for Lunenburg. Department Heads were
consulted for their recommendations concerning specialized needs in the school. These suggestions were considered
by the Building Committee and the Architect and, where feasible, were incorporated into the plans. These plans were
submitted to the School Building Assistance Bureau early in March and, after minor changes were made, the Building
Committee was authorized by the Bureau to proceed with
final plans and specifications. Progress during the plan-
ning stage was steady, and final construction plans were
completed and submitted to the School Building Assist-
ance Bureau in late April and approval was given by the
State in May. Specifications were then prepared and
bids solicited. The general bids were received and
opened June 1, 1967 and the building contract was
awarded to John J. Tocci and Sons, Inc. of Watertown,
Massachusetts and the contract document was signed on
June 21, 1967.
Ground breaking ceremonies took place at the
site on July 7, 1967 with members of the Building Com-
mittee, Superintendent of Schools, School Committee
members, Town officials and representatives of the Stoner
Associates, and Tocci and Sons, Inc., present. After
checking qualifications and references of four applicants,
the Committee unanimously selected Mr. Carl Moberg
of Worcester as Clerk of the Works.
Construction progress has been hampered by the
weather and the steel strike. It is anticipated, however,
School Bldg. Committee: Robert H. Zeidler, Dr. CyrilA. Bergman,
that the school will be ready for occupancy in September
Martha J. Johnson, Stanley H. Howard, Daniel P. Craffey.
of 1968.
