MIDDLE SCHOOL PERSPECTIVE
===PAGE 90===
Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical High School
The purpose of this report is to provide a summarized record of the major activities and developments of this past
year. Our School Committee worked diligently and faithfully to insure that the youth and adults of the Montachusett
School District will have a facility that will provide the highest quality Vocational and Technical education. Con-
siderable progress has been made toward the consummation of plansand hopes for the new Vocational-Technical School.
The structure will be designed for 1,000 students with core facilities that will make expansion possible to eventually
house 1,500.
On January 4, a Superintendent Rating Board was organized, with Mr. Richard Dunn, Chairman, Mr. R owland
E. Bonner, and Dr. Clarence P. Quimby, members. Subsequently, 26 applications were received and were qualified
by the Bureau of Vocational Education in Boston. After interviews, oral and written examinations, the Committee
elected Mr. George L. Ross, Superintendent-Director, effective July 1, 1967. The formation of the Montachusett
Regional-Vocational Technical School District was validated by an act of Legislation and signed by Governor Volpe
on June 12, 1967.
The "Business and Industrial Advisory Committee" was organized on February 1st, and held its first meeting
on February 8th. Its concept and function is to promote school, employer and union relationships to provide recommend-
ations for course offerings and adult training and re-training programs, and to evaluate the need for initiating pro-
gram changes. This committee has aided in developing survey as well as being instrumental in initiating an industrial
public relations program. As an additional aid for the design of shops, laboratories, and facilities, 15 craft committees
were organized. Their membership consists of a cross representation of specific skill areas. These include employers
of large and small companies, unions, engineers, and technicians. The craft committeesare of invaluable aid in recom-
mending equipment, course content, and in keepingpa with technological advances. Their long range function will
be to maintain school-craft relations and to provide clinics and demonstrations supplemental to the classroom.
The School Committee has adopted a part of its philosophy "a program of instruction that will be updated and
flexible, provide for career education and be readily available to people of all ages and needs". It plans to operate 12
months a year, 6 days a week and evenings, and expects to include occupational courses aimed at the general course
student, as well as those with technical level potential. It will have adult training and retraining programs. Thus it
will provide for upgrading skills for those already in industry, aswell as benefiting the under-employed socio-economi-
cally disadvantaged. Based on this philosophy, "Montachusett Regional" and the State Board of Education's Vocational
Bureau conducted series of surveys and studies to determine the curriculum. Twenty-eight courses were thus determined
as the most essential and promising for career orientation in this area. The program will consist of Grades 9 through 12
and will include post graduate courses. The courses offered beyond the 12th grade will include beginning skills as
well as advanced courses in depth. The same facilities that are designed for the day students will be used for 2000 or
more adults and out of school youths in the late afternoon, evenings, Saturdays and during the summer.
At the request of the town of Westminster, early in the year, an amendment to the Agreement was drafted per-
mitting Westminster's withdrawal from the District. The amendment was submitted to, and approved by, the towns and
cities.
At the first meeting in January, and again in April, at the annual reorganization meeting, Mr. Richard Dunn,
Chairman, and Mr. Francis A. Carbone, Vice-Chairman, were elected unanimously. Mr. Morrill G. Sprague was re-
appointed as Treasurer, and Mr. Victor Warner was reappointed as Secretary. Mr. Bradstreet had resigned as Chair
man to accept the position of Director of Guidance and Curriculum Development, effective January 1, 1967. Mr.
Herbert N. Hoffman was appointed to take Mr. Bradstreet's place as Lunenburg's representative on the Regional School
Committee.
On April 5th, the School Committee voted to obtain approval for incurring debt up to $150,000 for the purpose
of acquiring a site; preparing architectural and engineering plans; and for the construction of a Vocational-Technical
School. This action was approved by the member communities and the Massachusetts Emergency Finance Board.
- 88 -
===PAGE 91===
The District entered into a formal contract with the firm of Korslund, LeNormand & Quann, Inc., as the Archi-
tects. The District then voted to incur a debt of $65,000 for acquiring a site, and for preparing engineering and
architectural plans for the construction of this school. After examining and evaluating 12 sites, the Committee took
by eminent domain the site known as the Roper Estate and a parcel of land owned by Weyerhaueser Company on Route
2-A, near the Route 140 interchange. In selecting this site the Committee was motivated by its central location; the
ease of access to all of the towns and cities in the District; and by the comparatively lower cost of site development.
Since the signing of the contract with the architects, several schematics (based on the course offerings as prescribed
by the tentatively approved educational specifications) were received. On December 20th, the School Committee
approved a final schematic and the architect was instructed to develop the plans.
At the December 6th meeting, a final operating budget for 1968 was adopted in the amount of $53,410 less re-
imbursements. The anticipated revenue from the Federal government for salaries (from P.L. 88-210) is in the amount of
$12,000, thus reducing the budget to a total of $41,410, Lunenburg's share of this amount being 9. 34% or $3,867.69.
As a result of the passage of Senate Bills # 1410 and 1412, 50% reimbursement for all operating expenses for Vocational
schools was instituted, making it possible to educate students at the Regional Vocational School at a much lower student
rate. Savings to participating Communities should be reflected in a lower student cost than the per student cost of their own
schools. In addition, the communities which upto now had not participated in Regional School Districts, will be eligible
for an additional 15% of their State annual reimbursement.
The time schedule as originally developed in February 1967 and revised in September, to include more detail,
indicates that the start of construction should take place during the Fall of 1968, subject to the approval of the Bond
Issue by the participating communities in the Spring of 1968. If construction can start as per schedule, the school can
open in September 1970.
The Committee has accepted the following co-educational Curriculum:

School Housing Study Committee
The projections which have been established by previous Study Committees were updated using the same formula.
The figures still continue to show an increase but more gradual than the long term trend line shown in previous reports.
It is now estimated that there will be 2500 students in the Lunenburg School System by the year 1975.
The Committee feels continual efforts should be made to watch the population growth in the surrounding areas.
We can be seriously affected with the expansion of the Route 495 area and the possibility of a North-South Highway.
Lunenburg is situated within easy commuting distance to both of these areas.
Our Kindergarten presently estimated at an enrollment of 150 children and the uncertainty of the Regional Tech-
nical High School situation are two factors which could change our requirements considerably.
Our recommendation is for the continuation of a School Housing Study Committee to watch changes in trends,
record them against projections and make recommendations to the proper town officials.
Members of School Housing Study Committee from left to right: John Pieksen, Ralph Maki, William Steele,
George Larkin, William Mason, William Bartlett and Howard Brock.

===PAGE 95===
School Committee
Superintendent's Annual Report
Over the period of the past five years, the annual report of the Superintendent of Schools has
been submitted as an all inclusive report incorporating the activities of all of the schools. In an
effort to vary its style somewhat this year, separate reports have been written by the Principals con-
cerning their respective schools. My portion of the narrative report will, therefore, be somewhat
more brief and general in nature in an effort to avoid duplication.
1967 started off educationally on a positive note, for after much soul-searching and struggling
within ourselves as a Community, the citizens at a Special Town Meeting in January, overwhelmingly
voted to build a much needed new school. This action represented a significant forward step and was
found to have a profound influence upon the future of education in Lunenburg, and many of our efforts
were devoted to fulfillment of this project during the year. Since the approval of this new school
signalled the eventual end of improvising and squeezing and afforded the opportunity for long delayed
program improvements, every effort was made to expedite its progress. Firm bids for school construc-
tion were received in May and groundbreaking took place in early June.
These were encouraging steps, yet much work remained to be done, for plans had to be devel-
oped to determine how best to implement this school as well as to crystallize the changes which
would result in the present schools as a result of this new facility. Perhaps, we should first answer
a few questions about just what a Middle School is.
- 93 -
===PAGE 96===
What is A Middle School?
Actually, there are very few prototypes of Middle Schools in our country to emulate, although
interest in them is growing at a rapid pace. A half dozen or more are currently in the planning stage
in Massachusetts with four or five in actual recent operation. Generally a Middle School is one
which would house students between the ages of ten (10) and fourteen (14). Such an age span provides
considerable latitude in the combinations of grade levels which may be grouped in such a school.
Possibly, the most popular grouping consists of Grades 6, 7 and 8, yet one of the unique
qualities of this school is its flexibility since it contains both self-contained classrooms and special-
ized areas which can meet the needs of pupils at the Elementary level as well as at the Junior High
level. In Lunenburg, because of the combined dictates of our school enrollment and the existing
schools, our Middle School will house initially four (4) sections of Grade 4 and Grades 5, 6 and 7.
With a facility having such specialized areas as Art, Music, Homemaking, Industrial Arts,
Science Labs, a Language Laboratory, Specialized Reading Lab and a sizeable library, it provides a
stimulating challenge to look at our educational program in the intermediate grades to see how we can
possibly innovate and improve our educational efforts and thereby capitalize on the learning opportun-
ities made possible.
The challenge seemed exciting ! The School Committee felt that such educational possibilities
in this school required that a Principal be selected as early as possible so that planning could proceed
forthwith. As a result, Mr. Floyd Barney was appointed to the Principalship with duties which began
in January of 1968.
At this point in time, decisions have been made that would lead to at least a movement away
from the traditional self-contained one teacher concept toward limited departmentalization, and ability
grouping by subject area rather than by a single group in all disciplines. French and Spanish will be
offered to all students at Grade 7 leading toward the concept that a second language is almost neces-
sary in today's world and an early start helps in acquiring mastery. Limited Industrial Arts, Home-
making, greater emphasis on Science also will be brought in at this earlier age. Continued refinement
of plans for the effective implementation of this school will proceed with Superintendent, Principal,
and the staff during the winter and spring months of 1968 so that the school can open with a meaning-
ful impact.
How the Opening of the Middle School Affects the
Rest of the School System
The Architect, Contractor and Building Committee have given reasonable assurance that the
Middle School will be ready for occupancy with the opening of school in September. Accordingly, the
School Committee has planned its programs in September 1968 with this thought in mind.
This housing relief provided by the new school, brings into focus many positive benefits for
the rest of the school system as well as in the Middle School.
To begin with, it means that there will no longer need to be rented classroom space, the
Elementary Cafeteria can be reopened and improvised spaces will no longer be necessary. These are
the obvious benefits, but there are also more important gains which are not so openly evident. What
are these? We must recognize that since we have not had sufficient educational space over the past
three years that we have been able to accommodate our students only by increasing class sizes and by
renting additional spaces. Such restrictions can result in oversized classes which inhibit meaningful
education and results in stifling limitations in progressive curriculum expansion as our enrollment in-
creases and as needs of the times dictate.
As a result, the School Committee has planned that:
(1) Our pupil-teacher ratio at all grade levels should be reviewed with the thought of
working toward goals recommended by the State Department of Education for quality
learning opportunities.
- 94 -
===PAGE 97===
(2) That attempts will be made to improve our curriculum offerings at the secondary
level so that we can more efficiently meet the needs of all of our students. It is
planned that a fourth year of Spanish, a Secretarial course, Accounting, Honors
English, Honors History and a second year of Electronics will be offered. In addi-
tion, Advanced Chemistry and Analytical Geometry and Calculus will be offered in
September 1968 if enrollment needs dictate. Such programs have been delayed be-
cause of space limitations; the possibility of their implementation gives us addi-
tional enthusiasm for future quality educational opportunities in our schools.
(3) and Thirdly, space will now be available in the Elementary School for the initiation
of public Kindergarten. During 1967, the State Department of Education completed
an inclusive report on the matter of public Kindergarten and came out with the result
that public Kindergarten will be mandatory by September, 1973.
This time delay in mandatory implementation was provided since the State recog-
nized that not all systems had sufficient space available. This would give them
time to acquire it. Since Lunenburg had made provision in its school planning for
Kindergarten, the School Committee has voted the public Kindergarten classes will
be opened in September of 1968 if the new school is to open on time. Currently,
about 60% of the children in Massachusetts have public Kindergarten opportunities
available, with the number increasing at a faster pace than ever before. About 160
boys and girls are expected to enroll in this program to be set up in six classes;
three (3) in the morning and three (3) in the afternoon.
It is expected that the foregoing positive effects of the opening of the Middle School will
result in a more effective educational system, but also, it is recognized that these actions willreflect
increased costs. It is our sincere hope that the results will be such that support will be as positive
as in the past since Lunenburg has continuously demonstrated its strong desire for quality education.
School Committee - Teacher Relations
Early in 1967, the School Committee signed its first contract with the Lunenburg Teachers
Association, which is affiliated with both the Massachusetts Teachers' Association and the National
Education Association. This action, resulted from the passage in late 1965 of Chapter 763 of the
General Laws which established the right of public employees of all types to organize and through
majority vote of the membership to select the organization they desired to represent them in Collective
Bargaining involving matters relating to Wages, Hours and Working Conditions. The teachers chose
to act in accordance with the provisions of this law, and the Lunenburg Teachers' Association was
lawfully elected as the representative bargaining group for all teaching personnel. During the fall of
1966 and in early 1967 much work and many meetings preceded the adoption of the above mentioned
contract which enumerated specific grievance procedures and provided a yearly opportunity to review
matters relating to salaries.
At a time when much dissention has been prominent between School Committees and organized
teacher groups throughout the country, our teachers and School Committee have continued to work
straightforwardly, effectively and harmoniously. Approximately two and one-half months of talking,
studying and negotiations during the year preceded final mutual agreement on matters relating to
salaries which become effective in September, 1968. The groundwork has been laid for more careful
and detailed refinement of items covered in the Contract. The outcome of this future matter of con -
sideration can lead to the strengthening of a good school system if the future relationships continued
to be maturely handled by both parties. This new dimension in School Committee-Teacher relations
has involved many hours of time in meeting as well as considerable study, research and additional
pressures on the part of School Committee members. One of my very real concerns is that the addition
of these responsibilities and increased demands will make it more and more difficult for qualified
School Committee members to find the time to give of themselves to the extent required. To date,
Lunenburg has in my mind been uniquely blessed with quality school committee members who have
been outstanding in their unselfish devotion to the assignment.
- 95 -
===PAGE 98===
The climate of mutual respect between the School Committee and all personnel is such that
continued cooperation towards improved education should be inevitable in Lunenburg.
National Seminar on Educational Innovations in Hawaii
In June 1967, Lunenburg was selected as one of about twenty school systems in the State to
participate in a conference on educational innovations held in Hawaii for a period of eight days during
the month of July. This conference was jointly sponsored by the Kettering Foundation and the United
States Office of Education and brought together many of the most outstanding scholars from all over
our country, Canada and England. Lunenburg was selected as a participant because of the somewhat
unique pre-school program it had initiated as a result of seeking and receiving a federal grant under
Title III of the Elementary and Secondary Education Act. This program was started in October of 1966
and carried on through June of 1967. It was granted funds for a second year of operation in the summer
of 1967 and is currently in operation. The objective back of this program is to screen all pre-school
children by means of interview, testing, observation and any other means in an effort to discover any
pupils who might potentially have considerable difficulty for one or several reasons when they enter
school for the first time. Finding these an attempt is then made to find ways of helping them over-
come these difficulties before they become too strongly entrenched. We started out with approximately
160 pre-schoolers and ended up with about 30 that we spent some months with during the intervention
process. Our results to date have not been exceptional, yet they have been sufficiently positive that
we hope to have a significant contribution to make toward educational innovation. We are continuous-
ly receiving inquiries as well as visits from many areas of the State and Country seeking information
on this Project.
It was my privilege to represent Lunenburg at this important conference where all types of
innovative ideas in education were exposed. It was an experience which made me keenly aware of
the need to constantly improve in education if we are to provide the types of opportunities so necessary
in today's world.
The Cost of Education
Like practically all phases of life, education continues to become more costly. This is
largely due to the fact that the demands are greater both in numbers of students and the sophistication
of learning. Salaries which long were low have moved rapidly forward in recent years and the demands
for better educated teaching staff have increased immeasurably. Along with these facts, ever increas-
ing inflation has taken its toll. We are told that what cost ten dollars ($10.) only a year ago would
now cost eleven dollars and twenty-seven cents ($11.27). To improve then continues to take in-
creasingly greater effort.
Lunenburg, through its School Committee, has strived generally to be at least at the median or
above in its support of its educational efforts. With the standardization of school budgeting through-
out the country within the past two years, it is now possible to make some valid comparisons which
follow:
Since the area of Instruction is our main product, I would like to direct attention to this
specific portion of our effort. School Management, a professional education magazine, reports in
January, 1968 that the National Median reflected in expenditures for Instruction in 1967 amounted to
78% of the total budget. Lunenburg's effort in this area amounted to 74.8%, slightly below the median.
This figure reflects expenditures for all instructional salaries, Principals, Teachers, Clerical and
Coaching personnel. Analysis of this shows that we are not slightly below the median because of
inadequate competitive salary schedules, but rather more because of not having been able to provide
more in numbers of personnel because of limited space facilities. This is one of the areas where im-
provement will be made with the opening of the new school as has already beenreflected in this report.
In the important area of Textbooks, the National Median was $5.10 for each student while
Lunenburg was somewhat above this figure at $5.61 per student. In the areas of Administration,
Operation and Maintenance of Plant, we were generally at or somewhat below the median.

Conclusion
It takes the cooperative efforts of many people to make a school system truly meaningful and
effective. Basic, of course, is the parent and taxpayer who wants, expects and is willing to support
quality education. An enlightened, dedicated School Committee endowed with perseverance and big-
ness of mind is essential to establish realistic goals and to insist upon their implementation. Basic
also, of course, is the quality, interested teacher who does the teaching job on the firing-line each
day directed by enlightened leadership of Principals and supervisory personnel. Efficient clerical
personnel expedite smooth operation while custodians provide an inviting environment in which to
work.
The cooperation of all of these people is exemplary in Lunenburg and their combined efforts
have contributed toward making the Lunenburg Public Schools a symbol of pride and achievement.
Respectfully submitted,
RALPH W. BROWN,
Superintendent of Schools

Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School
Lunenburg Junior-Senior High School opened this year with an enrollment increase of fifty-four
students above the previous year. Because of the removal from the building of two sixth grade class-
es the demands on our instructional facilities became no greater than they were in 1966-67, at which
time the school operated above capacity.
With the anticipated opening of the new Middle School next September we expect that the secon-
dary school program (8-12) will become more effectively organized and implemented, with an occupancy
of eight hundred students. This reduced number of students will allow more flexible scheduling with
maximum use of special areas because of the absence of restrictions caused by above normal enrollment
for the facility.
Among the many items purchased this year for instructional improvement through State and Federal
Education Funds was a Steno-trainer for use in the business education department. This effective
piece of equipment enables a class of students studying shorthand to progress at individual and va-
ried speeds of dictation. Funds were also obtained for equipment and supplies for the science and
guidance departments and for the purchase of books for the school library. These available funds
importantly supplemented the Lunenburg School Department budget appropriation. Plans have been sub-
mitted for obtaining materials for several other academic departments for next year.
A summer study program was continued this
year for research, evaluation and recommenda-
tion for improvement of curriculum. This past
summer the area of English for grades one
through twelve was examined and a specific
course of study for all grades was developed
by teachers and consultants from various grade
levels. We hope to be able to continue this
cooperative curriculum project work each
successive summer until courses of study for
every academic area have been reduced to spe-
cific direction at all grade levels.
Some statistics of student study at Lunen-
burg Junior-Senior High School are interesting.

Every student finds it necessary to pursue at least five full credit courses each year as part
of the school requirements. It might be well, here, to review the requirements for graduation from
Lunenburg High School in addition to the aforementioned requisite.
FOR GRADUATION:
(1) The student must acquire eighteen Carne-
gie Units of Credit - one credit is granted for
the successful completion of each full time
course,
(2) The student must satisfactorily complete
four sequential years of English study - no
student may pursue more than one year of the
required English in any one year.
(3) The student must take two years of U. S.
History and receive credit with a passing grade
for at least one year.
(4) The student must pursue a course in art
appreciation and a course in music appreciation
during one of the years in high school.
(5) Exception to these requirements may be
made by the Principal for students not enrolled
for four years at Lunenburg High School and for
other reasons created by extenuating circum-
stances.

In closing I wish to congratulate the Lu-
nenburg School Committee and Superintendent
Brown for their appointment of Mr. Floyd F.
Barney as the Principal of the Middle School,
It has been my pleasure to have worked closely
for many years with this dedicated educator and
I sincerely wish him continued success in his
new venture, We have been fortunate indeed to
have had a person so well qualified and in-
terested in the educational development of the
young people of Lunenburg as Mr. Philip M.
Crouse, who has been elected to replace Mr.
Barney as the Assistant Principal of Lunenburg
Junior-Senior High School.
Respectfully submitted,
RICHARD J. CAREY, Principal

We all have to learn, in one way or another, that neither men nor boys get second
chances in this world. We all get new chances to the end of our lives, but not
second chances in the same set of circumstances; and the great difference between
one person and another is how he takes hold and uses his first chance, and how he
takes his fall if it is scored against him.
-THOMAS HUGHES

===PAGE 109===
Lunenburg Elementary Schools
There is widespread concern among Americans today about education. The rapid changes in modern
life impose new conditions with which all citizens, young and old, must learn how to deal. Know-
ledge is multiplying far beyond the limits of general comprehension. The introduction of new tech-
niques is making even recently acquired skills obsolete. Inherited modes of thought and conduct
have been displaced or are under challenge. Although greater demands have been made on the institu-
tions of higher learning in the fields of Science and Technology, gradual changes have to be made at
the elementary level to keep pace.
Here, in Lunenburg, the administration, teachers and supervisors have endeavored to continue to
examine and update the curriculum and introduce new techniques and services, which we hope will cul-
minate in a better understanding each child will have in the world in which he finds himself. We
have been somewhat handicapped in this respect by the overcrowded conditions at the elementary level.
It was necessary this year to find two more spaces to house first grade children as the high
school needed more classrooms which were occupied by our sixth grades. We were most fortunate in
being able to obtain two more classroom spaces in the United Parish Building, making four first
grade classes in this location. Although this greatly relieved the situation, one more space could
have been utilized for the first grade to bring the class sizes down to the number recommended by
the State Department of Education and our own local school committee. We have continued for the se-
cond year to house two sixth grades in the Elementary Cafeteria and one sixth grade at the Junior-
Senior High School.
In keeping pace with change, we have, during the past school year, implemented two new programs
and greatly improved upon a third which have been direly needed for some time. Although they have
been carried on in sub-standard quarters, we feel that they have been more than successful and a
great step forward in our educational system. These are in the area of Guidance, Speech and Hearing
Therapy, and improved Library services.
ELEMENTARY SCHOOL GUIDANCE
Guidance in the American Secondary School
has been developing for half a century but
guidance in the Elementary School is of more re-
cent origin. In the fall of 1964 the United
States Congress passed an important amendment
to the National Defense Education Act. This
amendment specifically supported Elementary
School Counseling.
Locally, our Elementary Guidance program
was initiated in September 1967. From the out-
set the objectives of the program were focused
on helping children to develop to their fullest
potential. In performing his role, the counse-
lor joins forces with other members of the
school team, teachers, administrators, nurses
and with all others interested in the welfare
of children.
Counseling is provided to assist children
in the normal process of growing up as they
seek to understand themselves, meet the develop-
mental tasks of childhood, learn effectively,
and develop realistic self understanding. Em-
phasis is on the child as a learner in the
Mr. John White, GUIDANCE COUNSELOR,
school situation, and the counselor is viewed
listens to child's problems.
by the child as a non-evaluative adult in a
helping relationship.
- 107 -
===PAGE 110===
Consultation is provided, which is the process of sharing with another person or group of per-
sons information and ideas, and of making mutually agreed upon decisions about the next steps needed.
The counselor, on occasion, meets with parents to discuss with and learn from them about their chil-
dren and offers them an opportunity to express their feelings about the child and his school situa-
tions.
Through coordination the school's total effort is brought into focus in the child's behalf,
eliminating duplication of effort and insuring follow-through on decisions made and policies esta-
blished. Effective coordination involves close working relationships between the teacher, counselor,
administrator, parent and other school community personnel whose contacts with the child in the
school situation are significant.
We have a young program but one which we feel will be of great help to all through continued
cooperation and the support of everyone. We are most fortunate in having as our Guidance Counselor,
Mr. John White, who has been outstanding in this new program. His skill, personal warmth and honest
liking for people have enabled him to establish excellent rapport with all those with whom he works.
SPEECH AND HEARING THERAPY
The Lunenburg Schools have been most for-
tunate in obtaining the services of a full-
time Speech and Hearing Therapist. Mrs. Sue
R. Durgin is a graduate of Bowling Green Uni-
versity, Bowling Green, Ohio. Mrs. Durgin's
experience in speech and hearing includes work
in the College Clinic at Bowling Green, con-
ducting a speech clinic for the Ohio Crippled
Children's Society in the summer of 1966,
Speech Therapy for the Westport, Connecticut
School System for the school year 1966-67;
and doing speech and hearing work for project
"Headstart" in Ohio.
The Remedial Speech Program in Lunenburg
thus far has consisted of identifying and
working on the speech problems of the children
in grades One through Twelve. As a result of
the conscientious efforts of the teachers, the
school nurses and the Guidance Counselor, all
children who manifest the slightest indication
of a problem are referred to the Speech Thera-
pist for a speech evaluation. It is felt that
close contact between parents, teachers, and
Second grade children receive help from
the therapy consultant is a definite asset to
Mrs. Sue Durgin, SPEECH THERAPIST.
a successful program. Children who partici-
pate in the program have problems ranging from simple articulation disorders to stuttering and hear-
ing problems. It is hoped that these children will no longer have to labor under the handicap of a
speech and hearing impairment in attaining the education they so richly deserve.
ENGLISH CURRICULUM STUDY
During the summer of 1967 the English Curriculum Study Committee formulated a language program
for Grades One through Twelve which had as its basis the work being done presently throughout the
grades. The current texts were used as guide lines. The Committee put the curriculum into written
form so that the Junior High teachers, for instance, would be cognizant of the work in the sixth
grade; so the ninth grade teachers would be cognizant of the work in the Junior High; and, converse-
1y, so that the fifth grade teachers would be aware of the requirements for the sixth grade.
Some of the committee's major concerns were: continuity of work through the grades, uniformity
of material covered within grade levels, and an awareness of the relationship of each grade's pro-
gram to the total English program. The committee considered literature on the elementary level and
recommended a greater emphasis in Grades Four through Six. Also, it was necessary to attempt to as
sure sufficient practice in written composition in every, grade.
- 108 -
===PAGE 111===
In the elementary grades it was evident that continuity of subject matter needed to be esta-
blished. With a written curriculum everyone has a frame of reference and it is much easier to in-
troduce a new teacher to the English curriculum for her grade level.
The committee discovered that there was some unnecessary duplication of subject matter in
Grades Two through Six. When this was the case, the responsibility for the material was assigned
to a particular grade level, and reteaching, as necessary, was assigned at other grade levels.
Details which help to establish the importance of English in the minds of pupils are neat pa-
pers and corrections for the pupils to see and understand. Standard forms for written work were
included and a list of correction symbols was included in the curriculum.
LIBRARY SERVICES
For the first time in the history of the
Lunenburg Elementary Schools, we have had the
services of a librarian this year for three
days a week. We are fortunate in having the
services of a most qualified librarian, Mrs.
Eleanor Whitcomb. In the past, the work has
been done by the Reading Supervisor and volun-
teer mothers whose services have been greatly
appreciated.
It is the purpose of the Elementary School
Library to furnish educational material in ad-
dition to the regular textbooks available, and
books that are worthwhile and fun to read. The
emphasis for the younger children is on the
pleasure reading can bring. There are two
types of books for this age group - picture
books which are self-explanatory, and the be-
ginning readers to give the younger child the
pleasure of a book he can read by himself. The
upper elementary students need books to aid
Children are helped in the selection of books
them with their classroom work. Reference
by SCHOOL LIBRARIAN, Mrs. Eleanor Whitcomb.
books are used to answer specific questions
and to furnish background material.
duction on how to use the library is given at
the beginning of the school year. The emphasis
with this age group is on individual help.
The librarian works very closely with
the teachers in finding material helpful to
them, or in working with their classes on
special assignments.
We have been fortunate the past two years
to receive federal funds under Title II (ESEA)
to purchase books and audio-visual material
for the library.
SCIENCE PROGRAM
As we stated in the Superintendent's re-
port for 1965, a curriculum study was set up
in the Science area with the express purpose
of reviewing the science offerings in Grades
One through Twelve in an effort to determine
how we could improve Elementary Science offer-
ings and better articulate all science teach-
ing throughout the school system. The Com-
Mrs. Janet Goodrich, fifth grade teacher,
mittee completed its initial study and after
gives instruction on the use of equipment in
reviewing several texts presented its recom-
the new portable SCIENCE Laboratory.
- 109 -
===PAGE 112===
mendation which was adopted. The present school year saw the final implementation of texts in Grades
One through Six. Obviously the science curriculum involved a great deal more than using a basic set
of text books. Curriculum includes all aspects of the science program, such as the scope and se
quence of the text, activities, materials, equipment and audio-visual aids.
After the adoption of the type of program needed and the final implementation of the text, we
are now concentrating on equipment, materials and audio-visual aids, During the 1966-67 school year,
we purchased through the National Defense Education Act such equipment as portable laboratories
fully equipped, elementary microscopes, specimens, science transparencies, etc. With a long range
plan submitted to the State Department of Education as a result of the Science Study, major equip-
ment and materials will continue to be purchased under the National Defense Education Act for the
next two years to bring our science equipment up to an adequate level.
On behalf of the Elementary School personnel, I wish to commend the citizens of Lunenburg for
the continued support they give and the pride they take in their school system. I also wish to
thank the Staff, Superintendent of Schools, and the School Committee for their fine spirit of co-
operation and understanding which is always present.
Respectfully submitted,
RICHARD H. DUSTIN, Principal
A Man's Mind may be likened to a garden, which may be
intelligently cultivated or allowed to run wild; but whether cul- -
tivated or neglected, it must, and will, bring forth. If no useful
seeds are put into it then an abundance of useless weed-seeds,
will fall therein, and will continue to produce their kind.
Just as a gardener cultivates his plot, keeping it free from
weeds, and growing the flowers and fruits which he requires, SO
may a man tend the garden of his mind, weeding out all the wrong,
useless, and impure thoughts, and cultivating toward perfection
the flowers and fruits of right, useful, and pure thoughts. By pur-
suing this process, a man sooner or later discovers that he is the
master-gardener of his soul, the director of his life. He also re-
veals, within himself, the laws of thought, and understands, with
ever-increasing accuracy, how the thought-forces and mind-elements
operate in the shaping of his character, circumstances, and destiny.
- JAMES ALLEN
A teacher who can arouse a feeling for one single good
action, for one single good poem, accomplishes more than he
who fills our memory with rows on rows of natural objects,
classified with name and form.
- JOHANN WOLFGANG VON GOETHE

===PAGE 122===
School Nurse
The Town of Lunenburg employs two full time registered Nurses, Mrs. Doris M. Lahti and Mrs.
Gladys V. Barney, who do Public Health Nursing as well as the School Nursing.
During 1967, 244 visits were made to parents of school children for: follow up on some physi-
cal defects found by the School Doctor, vision and hearing failures, health supervision and absences
from school. If absolutely necessary, transportation was arranged to doctors, dentists and special
clinics.
The State requires all students, in competitive sports and Grades 4, 7 and 10, have annual physi-
cal examinations. Many of these physicals are done by students own family doctor; the rest are done
by School Physician, Dr. John DeLeo.
The State of Massachusetts set up a measles immunization program this year in an attempt to im-
munize all children who had not had the disease. One dose is presumably life long protection.
Through the Nashoba Associated Board of Health, the vaccine was offered to school children in Grades
1 through 6. A total of 213 children received the measles vaccine, most of the others who had not
had measles have previously been protected by their own doctor or in the Lunenburg Well Child Clinic.
In December of 1967, a law became effective in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts which requires
every school child - Kindergarten through Grade 8 - to be immunized against Small Pox, Diptheria,
Tetanus, Pertussis (Whooping Cough), Measles and Polio, unless exempted for Medical or religious rea-
sons.
If a school child has not had above immunizations, the parents will be asked to have family
physician sign a slip saying immunizations will be given. It is the feeling of the Nursing Staff
that, with few exceptions, our school children have received all of these valuable immunizations.
Our school system has been most fortunate in being able to keep immunizations at a very high
level through the programs offered each year by "The Nashoba Board of Health" as follows:
Diptheria and Tetanus Boosters and re-vaccination against Smallpox - Grades 1, 6 and 11.
Grades 1 only - Trivalent Oral Polio to children who have not previously had vaccine and a
Booster dose to those who have already completed series.
Tine Testing for Tuberculosis is administered to Grades 1 and 9 with follow up on any posi-
tive reactors and their families.
Dental Fluoride Treatments to Grades 2, 5 and 8 given by Mrs. Clarence May, Dental
Hygienist.
All the above programs require signed permission from parent and are certainly well accepted
with few refusals.
Vision and hearing testing are done on all students annually. Notices of failures are mailed
home to parents with a doctor's form attached. This form is to be returned to the nurse, so that the
school may best follow your doctor's recommendations in helping your child.
We are grateful for the cooperation of the School Superintendent, Principals and all other
school personnel, without whose cooperation it would be impossible to have our health programs so
successfully carried out.
Respectfully submitted,
DORIS M. LAHTI, R. N.

School Lunch Program
My fifth annual report of the School Lunch Program covers the school year, September 1966 to
September 1967. Due to the closing of the cafeteria at the Thomas C. Passios Elementary School, our
program was substantially reduced. Though lunches are no longer available, the Special Milk Program
is in effect there as well as at the Annex and two elementary classes at the United Parish Building.
110,587 paid "Type A lunches" were served
to high school students as well as 3,025 free
lunches to high school workers and the needy.
6,696 adult lunches, including those of the
staff, were also served. There were 172 lunch
days, making an estimated 700 lunches served
daily. 233,282 half-pints of milk were sold
and 11,598 half-pints of free milk were provid-
ed under the United States Department of Agri-
culture Special Milk Program as well as 6,545
half-pints sold to adults.
Financially, it was not a profitable year.
Food costs continue to climb and we received
over $6,000.00 less in Federal Commodities than
in the previous year. The opening balance for
the school year in September 1967 was $3,698.97
PASTE
401
after all bills were paid in June. But there
TOMATO
TON
was an outstanding reimbursement check for June
in the amount of $1,123.51 which has since been
received. Hopefully, we have been promised
more commodities for the 1967-68 school year.
Our program is required to operate on a non-
profit basis and we hope to maintain our pre-
sent meal cost to students @ 25c. With the ex-
ception of the School Lunch Supervisors' sala-
ry and fuel and electric costs, the program is
"GETTING READY FOR THE HUNGRY TROOPS"
self-supporting.
(L-R: Mrs. Ellen Brown, Mrs. Anita Forest
Participation percentage-wise for the Jr.-
and Mrs. Gloria Beaulac)
Sr. High School was 70% according to attendance.
The annual Spring School Lunch Workshop was held at the Nipmuc Regional High School in Mendon
during the April vacation. Mrs. Lucy Chartrand and I attended. On Friday, November 3rd, in conjunc-
tion with the Teachers' Convention, our personnel attended the County School Lunch Meeting held at
the Forest Grove Jr. High School in Worcester. Mrs. Chartrand also attended the summer conference at
the University of Massachusetts from June 25th-June 30th.
Personnel in the Jr.-Sr. High School consisted of Mrs. Lucy Chartrand as the Cook; Mrs. Hazel
Whitney as the Assistant Cook; and Mrs. Gertrude Solovei as the Baker. The general workers were Mrs.
Mary Thibault, Mrs. Gloria Beaulac, Mrs. Mary Haueisen, Mrs. Anita Forest and Mrs. Genevieve Nass.
Mrs. Phyllis Colello worked part-time at the Thomas C. Passios Elementary School handling the milk
program there. It was with much regret that Mrs. Hazel Whitney's resignation was accepted in June,
Her years of service in School Lunch go back to the days of the West School.
In closing, I wish to express my thanks to Mr. Brown, the School Committee, also the Principals,
Town Personnel and Boards, Mr. Archambault and the Custodians, and particularly to the School Lunch
Personnel for their hard work and dedication.
Respectfully submitted,
(Mrs.) PHYLLIS W. PROUTY, Supervisor

REPORT OF TITLE III ESEA - P.L. 89-10 PROGRAM
I hereby submit the first annual report
on Title III ESEA for the year 1966-67.
At a time when education is commanding
National concern, Lunenburg applied for and
received a federal grant $27,570.81 to pursue
an idea to aid preschool children prepare for
the first grade. The importance and value of
such an idea is emphasized by the knowledge
that the Lunenburg project was one of only
three-hundred awarded the grant out of some
2,000 applicants from across the nation.
Many people contributed to the success of
the project. Among them were PTA mothers, stu-
"See What I Can Do"
dent teachers from the State College at Fitch-
Late morning activity, using his hands to
burg, and well-known consultants. Sixteen mem-
create something before the class.
bers of the Lunenburg Elementary staff contri-
buted to better education by helping to pre-
pare these children for school. Rounding out
the staff of professionals were eight social
workers from the Mental Health Clinic in Fitchburg. Much of the project's success came from the
willingness of the parents of some 100 children, who allowed their child to participate in this pro-
ject. It speaks well for the parents of Lunenburg and their dedication and interest in quality edu-
cation for their children.
Saturday morning Intervention Sessions were held from January to June for a number of children
who for one reason or another were considered unready for the first grade. Having had this addition-
al boost helped many of these children to acquire the necessary strength so vital to a successful
start in school.
It is as yet too early to determine the
full effect the project will have on these
children. However, there have been some
healthy signs emerging that indicate that the
program is having some positive effects.
We are very fortunate here in Lunenburg to
have had Federal financing for an educational
idea that was conceived locally and to be able
to nurture it into a strong trunk from which
better educational excellence will blossom and
grow. We are confident that with the continued
cooperation from the parents the project will
have its desired effect on all children both
in Lunenburg and across the nation.
1
DONALD V. THORNE
Project Director
II A Study In Concentration"
Before school activity aimed towards self-
direction using puzzles and books.
